Consciousness researchers need Spinoza | Jordi Galiano-Landeira » IAI TV
Jordi Galiano Landeira
4 minutes

The mind-matter problem still haunts us to this day. The question of how and why consciousness and the physical world interact and influence one another remains a mystery. In response to this problem, the philosopher Spinoza argued that nature is constituted by something more fundamental than either the mental or the physical: something that contains both. Philosopher Jordi Galiano-Landeira here argues that Spinoza’s philosophy is panpsychist – matter contains mind – and that integrated-information theory can help solve some of the remaining problems with panpsychism.  

In 2023, the Oscar-winning film Everything Everywhere All At Once became a worldwide sensation. Its surreal humour and roller-coaster pace, from slow and mundane at the beginning to frantic chaos, complemented an original sci-fi storyline. The protagonist, Evelyn Quan Wang, transforms from a woman trapped in experiencing a monotonous, bureaucratically suffocating life to one who experiences the vast multiverse of possibilities she could have lived.

I remember watching this film on a sweltering summer night by the Mediterranean coast, feeling both amused and overwhelmed by the sheer intensity of events unfolding “everything, everywhere, all at once.” This overwhelm was neither entirely good nor bad. The protagonist seemed to feel the same: full-blown existential overload. Spoiler alert: by the end of the film, she realizes that, despite her ability to inhabit infinite possibilities (with an infinitude of skills and superpowers), the most meaningful choice was to stay in one and fully experience it.

This brings us to our topic of discussion. Do you recall the scene where Evelyn finds herself in a universe where she is a rock? Did she not still seem to feel something in that state? Well, imagine a world where every single thing, from the tiniest speck of dust to the vast expanse of multiverses, exhibits some degree of consciousness, some form of subjective experience. While this might sound like a fantasy, it is actually a theory: panpsychism.

At first glance, panpsychism may seem like a stretch. Why do we need to posit that a rock is conscious? However, panpsychism tries to solve one of the most perplexing problems in neuroscience, psychology, and philosophy of mind: Why are we conscious, and how does subjective experience arise? This is what David Chalmers famously called the “hard problem of consciousness” in 1995. Years later, Chalmers himself suggested that solving it might require a radically unorthodox approach, even pointing towards panpsychism.

If consciousness is a fundamental property of reality, then it has always existed, overcoming the problem of its origin. However, we do not know yet why and how we are conscious. A specific variant of panpsychism, named micropsychism, proposes that complex forms of consciousness, such as those experienced by humans, are constituted by micro-elements of consciousness. The idea is that these micro-elements combine to form more complex structures, leading to macroconsciousness. This would explain how and why different entities, say, humans and rocks, possess different levels of consciousness (though, according to the film, perhaps not).  If this were the case, have we solved the hard problem? Not quite.

___

The term “information” refers to a reduction in uncertainty within a system.

I’m checking the actual article plus a primary source on Integrated Information Theory so I can separate the philosophy from what it would realistically mean for your ToE.
Direct answer

For your ToE, this is philosophically relevant but not scientific evidence.

Its significance is:

it offers one possible metaphysical interpretation of reality — that mind and matter are two aspects of something deeper — but it does not give you a tested physical result, a new equation, or a new discriminating experiment. The IAI article explicitly frames the move in Spinozist and panpsychist terms and ties it to Integrated Information Theory (IIT) as a way to address remaining problems for panpsychism.

So for your ToE, the right use of this is not:

    “this proves consciousness is fundamental,”

but rather:

    “this is a candidate philosophical layer for how mind and matter might relate if the physical theory eventually leaves that question open.”

Step-by-step explanation
1) What this article is actually arguing

The article argues for a Spinozist picture in which reality is grounded in something more fundamental than either the mental or the physical, and it presents this as a panpsychist-friendly route for consciousness research. It also suggests IIT may help with some of panpsychism’s unresolved issues.

In plain English:

    it is not mainly doing physics,

    it is doing metaphysics of consciousness.

That distinction matters.
2) Why this matters to your ToE at all

Your ToE is trying to understand whether apparently different domains of reality are really different faces of one deeper structure. That is why this article is relevant: it proposes a similar move, but for mind and matter instead of QFT, QM, GR, EM, and COSMO.

So the article matters because it asks:

could consciousness and physical reality both arise from one deeper substrate?

That kind of question fits your project’s general style, even though the article itself is not physics evidence.
3) The biggest implication for your ToE

The biggest implication is this:

your ToE may eventually need a position on whether consciousness is

    fully emergent from physical structure,

    fundamental in some sense,

    or one aspect of a deeper neutral substrate.

This article pushes strongly toward option 3.

That does not mean you should adopt it. It means the article identifies a question your ToE may eventually have to answer.
4) Where IIT changes the discussion

IIT is relevant because it tries to make consciousness more mathematically structured by linking it to integrated information and the causal structure of a system. The Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy summary describes IIT as starting from phenomenological axioms and tying the quantity of consciousness to integrated information.

That gives this article more than pure hand-waving. But it also creates problems:

    IIT remains highly controversial,

    critics have argued that later versions push toward panpsychist or idealist implications,

    and there is ongoing debate about whether the framework is empirically strong enough.

So for your ToE:

IIT is a possible formal bridge between consciousness-talk and structure-talk, but it is not settled science.
5) The deepest significance

The deepest significance for your ToE is this:

it suggests that “physics first, consciousness later” may not be the only possible ordering of explanation.

A standard scientific picture says:

    first explain matter,

    then derive life,

    then derive mind.

A Spinoza/panpsychism/IIT picture says:

    maybe consciousness or proto-experience is not a late accident,

    maybe it is built into the deeper structure of reality in some way.

That is a very strong philosophical claim.
Your ToE does not currently need it to explain the pillars you have been working on. But if you ever want the project to become a true “theory of everything” in the widest sense, then mind may become unavoidable.
6) What this does not give your ToE

It does not give:

    a new physical law,

    a new field equation,

    a new derivation route,

    or a new experimental discriminator.

It also does not solve the combination problem for panpsychism — the problem of how tiny bits of proto-consciousness would combine into unified human consciousness. The article itself suggests IIT may help here, but that remains a philosophical proposal, not a resolved scientific fact.

So this is not a new physics milestone for your repo.
It is a metaphysical option.
7) Where this fits in your current architecture

This fits best as a possible future epistemic / ontological layer, not as a current pillar result.

It is most relevant to:

    questions about observerhood,

    measurement,

    objectivity,

    and the mind–matter relation.

It is not primarily a new result for:

    PILLAR-QFT

    PILLAR-GR

    PILLAR-EM

    or your current bounded scalar route.

So for your ToE, this belongs more in:

    a philosophy appendix,

    an ontology note,

    or a future “observer / consciousness” seam,
    not in the current physics closure stack.

8) The best disciplined takeaway

The best disciplined takeaway is:

keep this as a candidate interpretive framework, not as a foundational commitment.

Why?

Because your project has been strongest when it does not overreach. Your canon already emphasizes:

    bounded closure,

    non-claim scope,

    and clarity before truth inflation.

So the safest and most useful move is:

    recognize that consciousness may eventually need a place in a wider ToE,

    recognize that Spinoza-style neutral-monist or panpsychist views are one serious philosophical option,

    but do not import them into the physics core unless the project gains real structural reasons to do so.

Bottom line

The significance for your ToE is:

this article gives you a possible philosophical answer to the mind–matter problem — that both mind and matter may be aspects of something deeper — but it does not add new physical evidence to your theory. Its value is that it highlights a question your ToE may eventually have to face: whether consciousness is emergent, fundamental, or aspectual. For now, it is best treated as a possible future interpretive layer, not as part of your current physics core.
